Yeah, I do. Especially before I meet with clients. I'll usually check the bathroom mirror, and then... I'll pull out my phone camera a few times, too. I end up trying to adjust the lighting, or maybe I'll change my collar and try to angle my face so the sagging isn't as obvious.

It hasn't stopped me completely. But I have been a lot slower getting out the door for client meetings lately because I get stuck checking the mirror. If you add up the extra time in front of the mirror in the morning, and then the times it crosses my mind during client meetings... maybe? It could add up to an hour, or maybe a little less. I just really haven't tracked it closely enough to give you a solid yes or no on that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this concern about your jaw has caused significant problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's completely upended my life. I still get through my work. But... it's definitely having an impact. Like I mentioned, I've been slower getting out the door for client meetings. And sometimes during those appointments, I realize I'm just less present because my mind is focused on my jaw. \n\nOutside of work, it's affected me a bit too. I've actually skipped a few art openings recently. I just... I didn't want to be seen, or risk being photographed. So, yeah, it is definitely causing some issues, even if I'm still functioning day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Is your main worry specifically that something is wrong with how your jaw or face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's just this nagging thought that I can't seem to shake, especially when I have to be face-to-face with clients. It just makes me feel uncomfortable and, I don't know, self-conscious. So it's distressing enough to be a real annoyance, for sure, even if it's not completely ruining my day.\n\nDoctor: On an average day, about how much time do you spend thinking about your jaw or checking it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about an exact number of hours or anything. I haven't timed it. I just know it takes me longer to get ready in the mornings because I'm checking the mirror, and then it pops into my head during the day, especially if I'm meeting with someone. So, it's a fair amount of time, but I couldn't give you a specific number.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, do thoughts about your jaw take up more than an hour in total?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's tough to say for sure. If you add up the extra time in front of the mirror in the morning, and then the times it crosses my mind during client meetings... maybe? It could add up to an hour, or maybe a little less. I just really haven't tracked it closely enough to give you a solid yes or no on that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this concern about your jaw has caused significant problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's completely upended my life. I still get through my work. But... it's definitely having an impact. Like I mentioned, I've been slower getting out the door for client meetings. And sometimes during those appointments, I realize I'm just less present because my mind is focused on my jaw. \n\nOutside of work, it's affected me a bit too. I've actually skipped a few art openings recently. I just... I didn't want to be seen, or risk being photographed. So, yeah, it is definitely causing some issues, even if I'm still functioning day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Is your main worry specifically that something is wrong with how your jaw or face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's really what brings me in. I just keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and on one side of my face. It really started standing out to me after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos recently. I just can't stop focusing on how it looks, even though my wife—and even a couple of clients I've mentioned it to—say they barely notice anything at all. But to me, it just looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: Has this worry about your jaw made you avoid important activities or people because of how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a minute ago, it's mostly been those art openings that I've skipped recently. I just really didn't want to be seen or end up in the background of someone's photos. As for people... I haven't completely avoided my clients, since I still have to get through my work. But, yeah, I'm definitely slower getting out the door to go see them, and I'm just a lot less present during the actual appointments because I'm worrying about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I get frustrated and a bit down when I'm really focusing on how my jaw looks, sure. But I don't feel depressed most of the day or anything like that. My mood is generally fine otherwise.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been preoccupied with the way your jaw looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure on a specific timeframe, to be honest. Like I mentioned, it really just started bothering me after I saw those recent family photos. Before that, I hadn't really paid much attention to it, so... yeah, I couldn't give you an exact date or number of weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even my clients at work don't seem to see it, but... I just can't seem to let it go, and it's really starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or examining your jaw or face in mirrors, photos, or other reflections?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially before I meet with clients. I'll usually check the bathroom mirror, and then... I'll pull out my phone camera a few times, too. I'll just be standing there comparing my jaw to theirs instead of actually focusing on the conversation I'm supposed to be having.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unevenness in your jaw is probably minor or not noticeable to other people, or are you convinced it is clearly abnormal?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean, I hear what my wife says about not noticing it, so... maybe it is minor to other people. But when I look in the mirror or at those photos, it just seems so obvious to me. I guess I'm just not sure what to think about how bad it actually is to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Has worrying about your jaw or face been interfering with your work, social life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I still get through my work. It hasn't stopped me completely. But I have been a lot slower getting out the door for client meetings lately because I get stuck checking the mirror. And then during some of those appointments, I'm just... less present, I guess. Like my mind is elsewhere. \n\nOutside of work, it's definitely affecting things a bit. I've actually skipped a few art openings recently. I just didn't want to be out there being seen, or risk being photographed.\n\nDoctor: Aside from concerns about your jaw and face, do you have any other repetitive worries or rituals that feel hard to control?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just this nagging thought that I can't seem to shake, especially when I have to be face-to-face with clients. It just makes me feel uncomfortable and, I don't know, self-conscious. But, yeah, I'm definitely slower getting out the door to go see them, and I'm just a lot less present during the actual appointments because I'm worrying about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Even my clients at work don't seem to see it, but... I just can't seem to let it go, and it's really starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or examining your jaw or face in mirrors, photos, or other reflections?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I catch myself comparing my face to the younger advisors at the firm, or to other people when I'm out at gallery events. It hasn't stopped me completely. But I have been a lot slower getting out the door for client meetings lately because I get stuck checking the mirror. I've actually skipped a few art openings recently. I just didn't want to be out there being seen, or risk being photographed.\n\nDoctor: Aside from concerns about your jaw and face, do you have any other repetitive worries or rituals that feel hard to control?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's really just the stuff with my jaw and face. I don't have any other habits or worries like that.\n\nDoctor: How distressing does thinking about your jaw feel to you?\n\nPatient: It's... I mean, it's definitely frustrating. I wouldn't say I'm in absolute agony over it or anything, but it really bothers me. It's just this nagging thought that I can't seem to shake, especially when I have to be face-to-face with clients. It just makes me feel uncomfortable and, I don't know, self-conscious. So it's distressing enough to be a real annoyance, for sure, even if it's not completely ruining my day.\n\nDoctor: On an average day, about how much time do you spend thinking about your jaw or checking it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about an exact number of hours or anything. I haven't timed it. I just know it takes me longer to get ready in the mornings because I'm checking the mirror, and then it pops into my head during the day, especially if I'm meeting with someone. If you add up the extra time in front of the mirror in the morning, and then the times it crosses my mind during client meetings... maybe? It could add up to an hour, or maybe a little less. But, yeah, I'm definitely slower getting out the door to go see them, and I'm just a lot less present during the actual appointments because I'm worrying about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. It's just this nagging thought that I can't seem to shake, especially when I have to be face-to-face with clients. It just makes me feel uncomfortable and, I don't know, self-conscious. So it's distressing enough to be a real annoyance, for sure, even if it's not completely ruining my day.\n\nDoctor: On an average day, about how much time do you spend thinking about your jaw or checking it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about an exact number of hours or anything. I haven't timed it. I just know it takes me longer to get ready in the mornings because I'm checking the mirror, and then it pops into my head during the day, especially if I'm meeting with someone. So, it's a fair amount of time, but I couldn't give you a specific number.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, do thoughts about your jaw take up more than an hour in total?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's tough to say for sure. If you add up the extra time in front of the mirror in the morning, and then the times it crosses my mind during client meetings... maybe? It could add up to an hour, or maybe a little less. I just really haven't tracked it closely enough to give you a solid yes or no on that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this concern about your jaw has caused significant problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's completely upended my life. I still get through my work. But... it's definitely having an impact. Like I mentioned, I've been slower getting out the door for client meetings. And sometimes during those appointments, I realize I'm just less present because my mind is focused on my jaw. \n\nOutside of work, it's affected me a bit too. I've actually skipped a few art openings recently. I just... I didn't want to be seen, or risk being photographed. So, yeah, it is definitely causing some issues, even if I'm still functioning day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Is your main worry specifically that something is wrong with how your jaw or face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's really what brings me in. I just keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and on one side of my face. It really started standing out to me after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos recently. I just can't stop focusing on how it looks, even though my wife—and even a couple of clients I've mentioned it to—say they barely notice anything at all. But to me, it just looks wrong.\n\nDoctor: Has this worry about your jaw made you avoid important activities or people because of how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a minute ago, it's mostly been those art openings that I've skipped recently. I just really didn't want to be seen or end up in the background of someone's photos. As for people... I haven't completely avoided my clients, since I still have to get through my work. But, yeah, I'm definitely slower getting out the door to go see them, and I'm just a lot less present during the actual appointments because I'm worrying about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I get frustrated and a bit down when I'm really focusing on how my jaw looks, sure. But I don't feel depressed most of the day or anything like that. My mood is generally fine otherwise.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been preoccupied with the way your jaw looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure on a specific timeframe, to be honest. Like I mentioned, it really just started bothering me after I saw those recent family photos. Before that, I hadn't really paid much attention to it, so... yeah, I couldn't give you an exact date or number of weeks.\n\nDoctor: About how long has this worry about your jaw been going on?\n\nPatient: Like I just mentioned, I really don't have an exact timeframe for you. It was just whenever I saw those family photos. I haven't really been keeping track of the exact weeks or anything since then, so I can't give you a specific number.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after those family photos, has the worry about your jaw been going on for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. It hasn't been that long. The photos were taken recently, so... it's definitely been less than six months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this concern about your jaw causes you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a lot of distress. I mean, it definitely bothers me, but... it's not like it's ruining my life or keeping me from doing my job. It just really bugs me when I think about it or happen to look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that the change in your jaw means you might have a medical problem?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I haven't really thought about it being a medical issue. It's just... it's just how it looks that bothers me, not that I think something is physically wrong with me, like a disease or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even my clients at work don't seem to see it, but... I just can't seem to let it go, and it's really starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or examining your jaw or face in mirrors, photos, or other reflections?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially before I meet with clients. I'll usually check the bathroom mirror, and then... I'll pull out my phone camera a few times, too. I end up trying to adjust the lighting, or maybe I'll change my collar and try to angle my face so the sagging isn't as obvious. \n\nAnd it's not just the checking. I catch myself comparing my face to the younger advisors at the firm, or to other people when I'm out at gallery events. I'll just be standing there comparing my jaw to theirs instead of actually focusing on the conversation I'm supposed to be having.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unevenness in your jaw is probably minor or not noticeable to other people, or are you convinced it is clearly abnormal?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean, it's definitely frustrating. I wouldn't say I'm in absolute agony over it or anything, but it really bothers me. So it's distressing enough to be a real annoyance, for sure, even if it's not completely ruining my day.\n\nDoctor: On an average day, about how much time do you spend thinking about your jaw or checking it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about an exact number of hours or anything.
